---
title: Base Six palette
date: 2023-03-18
---
The six base colours patterns are written in — the quantiser anchors of internal/colour (quantise.go), as a navigable palette. Each is a point on the Tartan Dictionary fine grid (ref = its level-ring-hue address); see research/colour-six-palette.

<span style="display:inline-block;width:1.1em;height:1.1em;background:#F7F7F7;border:1px solid #0003;vertical-align:middle"></span><span style="display:inline-block;width:1.1em;height:1.1em;background:#000000;border:1px solid #0003;vertical-align:middle"></span><span style="display:inline-block;width:1.1em;height:1.1em;background:#CC0000;border:1px solid #0003;vertical-align:middle"></span><span style="display:inline-block;width:1.1em;height:1.1em;background:#F2BF00;border:1px solid #0003;vertical-align:middle"></span><span style="display:inline-block;width:1.1em;height:1.1em;background:#006100;border:1px solid #0003;vertical-align:middle"></span><span style="display:inline-block;width:1.1em;height:1.1em;background:#2A418A;border:1px solid #0003;vertical-align:middle"></span>

Print it: [**the Base Six shade card (PDF)**](/colour/base-six/base-six-shades.pdf) — every shade as a 24 mm swatch with its code, name and the reference values to dye against. Or [**the woven shade card (PDF)**](/colour/base-six/base-six-shades-woven.pdf) — each dye beside its colour-corrected woven (2/2 twill) reading, the way the sample sheets print a palette.

These six colours are not a designer's choice but a statistical one: they are chosen to maximise explanatory power over the recorded corpus. Six anchors separate the patterns the dyers actually distinguished — fewer would collapse setts that differ on the loom, more would split shades no weaver treated as different roles.

<div style="display:grid;grid-template-columns:repeat(2,5.5em);gap:.5em;justify-content:center;justify-items:center;text-align:center;margin:.5em 0"><span style="grid-column:1/3;text-align:center"><span style="display:block;width:3em;height:3em;background:#F7F7F7;border:2px solid #000"></span><code>W</code> White</span><span style="text-align:center"><span style="display:block;width:3em;height:3em;background:#CC0000;border:1px solid #0003"></span><code>R</code> Red</span><span style="text-align:center"><span style="display:block;width:3em;height:3em;background:#F2BF00;border:1px solid #0003"></span><code>Y</code> Yellow</span><span style="text-align:center"><span style="display:block;width:3em;height:3em;background:#2A418A;border:1px solid #0003"></span><code>B</code> Blue</span><span style="text-align:center"><span style="display:block;width:3em;height:3em;background:#006100;border:1px solid #0003"></span><code>G</code> Green</span><span style="grid-column:1/3;text-align:center"><span style="display:block;width:3em;height:3em;background:#000000;border:1px solid #0003"></span><code>K</code> Black</span></div>

And plotted over the fine grid in three illuminance layers — light, mid and dark. Each wheel is one lightness layer plotted in OKLab's own dimensions: the angle is hue — red at the top, then clockwise through orange and brown, yellow, green, blue and purple, the wheel's jog order — and the radius is chroma, with rings every 0.05. The faint grey dots are the fine grid at that lightness; the red dashed line is the dyeable limit — at each hue, the strongest chroma any lightness in the layer's band reaches. Hover a dot for its code, name and hex.

## The shades

Each shade in OKLCh — lightness **L**, chroma **C** and hue **h** (degrees). The small number under each is that axis's fine-grid index: the lattice level, chroma ring and snapped hue angle that together make the shade's grid code.
